> **Implements Pillar**: Pillar 1 (Quiet Is Power) + Pillar 3 (The World Watches)

## Overview

The Ecological Disturbance system is a server-authoritative event bus and spatial signal field that records the ecological impact of every player action on the alien planet. When a squad member gathers a resource, sprints, or activates a light source, that action publishes a disturbance signal — position, magnitude, and type — that decays over time and merges spatially with nearby signals from other players. The system is automatic: players do not call into it directly; their gameplay actions drive emissions through the systems that own those actions (Resource Node for gathering, Player Controller for sprinting and lighting). Downstream consumers read the field differently — the Predator AI queries it to locate the squad's hottest hotspot and target there; the HUD aggregates field values into the squad disturbance meter and fires per-player spike alerts when individual emissions cross a threshold; bioluminescent flora interpolate their visual saturation against the local field value, producing the "the world is responding to you" read that anchors the game's identity. Without this system, the central hook — *the predator hunts you along the disturbance you create* — has no infrastructure to run on: the predator collapses to a generic patrol-and-chase AI, the world stops reflecting player choices, and the "quiet beats loud" tension has no enforcement mechanism. This GDD specifies the bus's behavior — what events look like, how they decay, how they merge, how the field is queried — but not the implementation pattern, which belongs to a future ADR. The disturbance field is **session-scoped**: it is created at run start and discarded at run-end, with no cross-run persistence, so every run begins from a clean field — serving Pillar 4 (Rounds Not Saves) [R16-I-8].

## Player Fantasy

> *"The planet keeps a ledger, and your name has just been written into it."*

The Ecological Disturbance system serves an indirect fantasy: the player is not someone who creates disturbance — they are someone who is being read by a world they did not realise was paying attention. The fantasy is **passive recognition**. The squad strips a node cluster near the cave mouth. Nothing hostile happens. The fern-stalks they passed on the way in are glowing a half-shade brighter than before, and none of them remember whether it was that bright when they entered. The action committed two minutes ago is being recorded in the world's appearance now. There is no event log, no notification. There is just the brightening.

This fantasy serves **Pillar 3 — The World Watches**. The disturbance system is the mechanism that makes the pillar real. Without it, "the world watches" is a tagline; with it, every flora node within range of a recently-gathered point is a small physical witness to what the squad has done. The pillar is delivered through the *visible aftermath* of player actions, not through immediate punishment. The squad reads the consequence in the environment before they read it on the HUD.

The fantasy also has a secondary register that this system enables but does not own: **the silence after the mistake** (Pillar 1 — Quiet Is Power). When a single sprint or a single careless gather pushes the squad's local field across a tier threshold, the lived experience is *knowing a threshold was crossed without knowing which one*. The cost is paid in the next thing that happens, not in this moment. The disturbance system creates this delayed-consequence pattern because field decay is non-instantaneous and predator response is non-immediate — there is always a window between cause and effect, and that window is where the squad lives most of the run.

Players never engage with the disturbance system as a mechanic. They engage with **its visible outputs**: brighter flora, a higher meter, a predator turning to face them. The system's fantasy is the cumulative weight of all those outputs feeling earned — every visible change traceable back to a specific squad action. **If a player ever sees the world brighten and cannot trace it to something they did, the fantasy is broken at the squad level — the squad collectively is responsible for any brightening they observe.** **Round-7c I-7b-3 individual-level qualification:** at the individual level, the traceability promise holds for a player observing brightening they themselves caused, but does NOT always hold for a player observing brightening caused by a squadmate (edge (b) — D.5 max-aggregation propagation) or by the same squad before the player's chunk streamed in (edge (c) — chunk stream-in into pre-disturbed zone). Both edges are accepted-as-designed. **Coverage scoping (round-22 I15 — the HUD GDD is now authored):** the HUD's per-player contribution display (HUD GDD, accepted 2026-07-03) **fully closes edge (b)** — a player can read whose live `playerT` is elevated and convert blame into fellowship information — but only **partially closes edge (c)**: the display is a *live* readout, so a zone that is bright from a fully-decayed emission or a departed/disconnected contributor shows no live attribution at stream-in time. Edge (c) therefore remains a playtest watch-item flagged by H.PB1 question 3 (and question 5 for edge (b)'s residual read). This qualification preserves the squad-level Pillar-3 commitment as load-bearing (Section B's binding contract) while accepting that the individual-level read of the same edges depends on the HUD's per-player contribution UI being shipped.

### Player Fantasy — Resolved Design Tensions (round 2 review)

Three design-decision tensions surfaced during round-2 review and resolved here so the Player Fantasy commitment above remains load-bearing:

- **Stationary emitters: Light attenuates to low-baseline; Sprint still escalates (now loudly).** Sprint and Light pulses fire at full magnitude only when the player has moved more than `EMITTER_MOVEMENT_DELTA_FLOOR` (default 1 stud) in the previous pulse interval; below that delta, magnitude is multiplied by `STATIONARY_EMISSION_FACTOR` (default 0.3). For **Light**: a stationary lantern at a crafting station produces pulses at `0.08 × 0.30 = 0.024`, settling at `≈ 0.272` (Calm-tier) — it decays toward a low-baseline contribution and does NOT silently accumulate to Hunt-tier. For **Sprint**: Player Controller's `SPRINT_HOLD_FLOOR = 0.09` dominates the `STATIONARY_EMISSION_FACTOR` attenuation — a stationary Sprint pulse is published at `max(0.09, 0.030) = 0.09`, not `0.030`. The hold-floor reflects that holding sprint stationary IS loud (Pillar 1 — Quiet Is Power). A stationary-sprinting player escalates to Hunt in ~15 s and reaches Retreat-tier in ~28 s — this IS a "specific squad action" with a traceable, intentional consequence. See C.1.1, D.7, G.5. Resolved Q9 (stationary-Light traceability); 2026-06-19 ruling applies to Sprint hold-floor (ED yields to PC). The traceability promise is preserved for both: Light's low-baseline is traceable to "lantern raised, not moving"; Sprint's Retreat-ward escalation is traceable to "holding sprint is loud."
- **Pre-disturbed flora moments belong to the level-design and narrative layer, not the disturbance system.** Section B's traceability promise applies to the live disturbance field. Art-bible §6.4 Beat 3 (and similar moments) must be authored as set-dressing with diegetic in-world cause (expedition logs, dead alien creature, claw marks), *not* by hand-setting flora to elevated `t` values that bypass the disturbance bus. The flora-rendering subsystem renders one visual state — the live `t` from the disturbance field. Story moments use diegetic props and lighting fixtures, not field-state injection. Art-bible §6.4 must be updated before any flora-rendering implementation work. Resolved Q10.
- **"No escape once seen" is the design intent.** Once the predator's locked attribution resolves to a specific player and that player is in Hunt or Retreat tier, sprinting is a commitment, not an escape — sprint emissions still fire, the field still rises. The squad's correct response is to stop, hide, and let the field decay. This is taught in onboarding via a low-stakes Calm-tier first-zone teaching beat (Player Controller GDD owns the beat; HUD GDD owns the "predator locked — sprinting worsens signal" indicator). The disturbance system itself does not implement chase-state suppression. Resolved Q11.
- **Playtest-watched fantasy-fidelity risks (round-3 review).** Two Section-B fantasy edges are accepted-as-designed but flagged for explicit playtest watch under H.PB1 and a future expanded survey:
  - **(a) Stationary-sprint Retreat-reach (UPDATED 2026-06-19 — ED yields; user ruling).** Q9 attenuation applies to stationary-sprint recurring pulses, but Player Controller's `SPRINT_HOLD_FLOOR = 0.09` dominates: a stationary Sprint pulse is published as `max(SPRINT_HOLD_FLOOR 0.09, MAGNITUDE_SPRINT_PULSE × STATIONARY_EMISSION_FACTOR 0.030) = 0.09` — ED's stationary attenuation is effectively overridden for Sprint (it still binds Light, which has no hold-floor). D.7 worked example shows the Hunt floor (0.65) crossed in **~15 s** and Retreat-tier (1.0 clamp) reached in **~28 s** of zero-movement-with-sprint-held (asymptote `S_inf = 0.09 / (1 − r) ≈ 1.993 → clamps 1.0`; series `S(N) = 1.993 − 1.893 × r^(N−1)` with coefficient `1.893 = S_inf − MAGNITUDE_SPRINT_PULSE = 1.993 − 0.10`; full derivation in D.7's stationary-sprint table). **Design intent (2026-06-19 ruling): holding sprint stationary IS loud — it drives toward Retreat, not "barely Hunt." This IS the intended traceable outcome.** The inattentive-player scenario (terrain-latched motor stuck against geometry; AFK-with-keyboard-weight) reaches Retreat in ~28 s — much faster than the old ~160 s, so the failure mode is more visible but also more legible as a "your input is causing this" signal. Other squad members will see Retreat-tier flora through D.5's max-aggregation propagation. **H.PB1 question 4 explicitly verifies this watch-item** (perceived fairness of stationary-sprint Retreat-reach), and test-pilots should ensure first-zone level design avoids high-friction terrain that traps held-sprint inputs against walls. The timescale to watch in playtest is now a ~28 s Retreat-reach, not the prior ~160 s barely-Hunt. **Round-22 B12 (round-21 panel finding on this ruling's second-order consequences — does NOT reopen the ruling):** the "holding sprint IS a choice" fairness framing assumes a continuous-effort input, which is true on keyboard/gamepad but FALSE on touch — PC C.1.3 makes sprint a **tap-toggle** on the primary platform, so a stationary touch player may be "holding" nothing (forgotten toggle, attention elsewhere); and there is no mechanical safety net between an AFK/inattentive player and a squad-wide Retreat in ~28 s. **Resolution (RATIFIED by user 2026-07-03 — adopted per the round-21 panel + creative-director recommendation, then explicitly approved):** (1) a **PC-owned stationary sprint auto-release forward obligation** (F.2a row 9, `ecological-disturbance-forward-obligations.md`) — sprint auto-releases after a PC-owned window (recommended 8–10 s) of near-zero XZ movement regardless of input mode, with a HUD pre-warning cue; deliberate players re-press and escalation continues across re-presses (field sources persist ~65 s), preserving the ruling's intent-gated loudness while bounding the unattended case below the ~15 s Hunt crossing; (2) **H.PB1/H.PB1-BETA question 4 data MUST be split by input method** (touch vs hold-input) so a touch-specific fairness failure cannot be pooled away — landed in Section H this round.
  - **(b) Squad max-aggregation propagation (D.5).** A single squad member at Hunt-tier propagates Hunt-tier visual response to all squad members' nearby flora via D.5's `max(playerT)` aggregation. Section B's "every visible change traceable to a specific squad action" is satisfied at the squad level (someone in the squad caused it), not always at the individual level (the player observing the brightening may not personally have caused it). This is an accepted Pillar-2 (Fellowship) trade — the squad shares the consequence of any member's action. **H.PB1 question 5 explicitly tracks** whether players experience this as fellowship ("our squad caused this") or as blame ("P1 caused this and now I'm in Hunt"); failure mode is the latter (>50% BLAME responses). Mitigation if observed: HUD GDD should explore showing per-player contribution in the squad meter UI.
  - **(c) Chunk stream-in into pre-disturbed zone (round-7a R7-IMPORTANT — game-designer F6 elevated).** When a player's StreamingEnabled radius newly streams in a chunk that already has an elevated `fieldValue` from emissions placed by a teammate (or by the same player) before the chunk was loaded on this client, the player's first visual-snapshot of that chunk shows Hunt-tier flora with no per-player traceability — the player cannot retroactively "see" the squadmate's earlier action that caused the field elevation, only its decayed-visual-residue. This is a Section-B traceability edge: at the squad level, traceability holds (the field elevation IS a squad-action consequence); at the individual level, the player observing the bright zone has no personal causal context. **CD round-7 finding (game-designer F6):** this is a Pillar 3 (The World Watches) traceability edge that is functionally identical to (b)'s individual-vs-squad trade, but specific to the StreamingEnabled / cross-chunk persistence path (C.1.10) where temporal separation amplifies the traceability gap. Accepted-as-designed (the alternative would be fading-in flora artificially when chunks load — a worse design that breaks "the field drives the visuals"). **Mitigation:** the same per-player contribution display in the squad-meter UI proposed for (b) addresses this edge **partially** (round-22 I15 scoping): it attributes the elevation only while the causing source is still live and its contributor still present — a fully-decayed or departed contributor shows no live attribution at stream-in time, so H.PB1 question 3 remains the load-bearing check for this edge. **H.PB1 question 3 (free-form) captures whether players whose first-encounter with a bright zone was via stream-in describe the experience as legible vs jarring.** If H.PB1-BETA shows >40% jarring responses, route to game-designer + level-design for first-zone authoring guidance (avoid placing player-spawn-points such that the first chunk-stream-in lands the player into a high-`t` zone driven by another squad's emissions).

These three edges are NOT design changes; they are watch-items recorded so they are not lost between this GDD's approval and vertical-slice playtest. Failures route through creative-director for design adjustment.

## Detailed Design

### Core Rules

**C.1.1 — What constitutes a disturbance emission.**
A disturbance emission is published exactly once per qualifying player action: (a) each individual resource-gather interaction on a node, at the moment the gather completes; (b) sprint-state entry (the first server frame a player transitions from walking to sprinting), and then once every `SPRINT_PULSE_INTERVAL` seconds thereafter for as long as sprint continues; (c) lantern raise (transition from lowered to raised state), plus once every `LIGHT_PULSE_INTERVAL` seconds while emitter brightness exceeds the ambient threshold; (d) crafting-station beacon activation (one large emission at activation; see C.3.6). No other actions currently publish. Actions that do not qualify (crouching, walking, looting a finished node) produce no emission.

**Stationary-emitter attenuation (resolves Q9; round-5 / Tier-2 #16 grace-window extension).** Sprint and Light recurring pulses are subject to a movement-delta gate. Player Controller samples the player's XZ position at each pulse-fire moment and compares against the position sampled at the previous pulse for that emitter. If the 2D distance moved is less than `EMITTER_MOVEMENT_DELTA_FLOOR` (default 1 stud, see G.5), the pulse fires with `initialMagnitude` multiplied by `STATIONARY_EMISSION_FACTOR` (default 0.3). **State-entry grace window (round-5 extension).** Any pulse fired within `FIRST_PULSE_GRACE_WINDOW` seconds (G.5; default 1.0 s) of state-entry — sprint start, lantern raise — fires at full magnitude regardless of movement-delta. At default tunings (`SPRINT_PULSE_INTERVAL = 2 s`, `LIGHT_PULSE_INTERVAL = 4 s`), the grace window covers exactly the first pulse on state-entry, so behaviour is unchanged. The grace window framing future-proofs against future pulse-interval reductions where multiple pulses could arrive within the first second of state-entry; without it, a future `SPRINT_PULSE_INTERVAL = 0.5 s` tuning would attenuate the second pulse on first sprint, reintroducing the "instant Tense on first sprint after gather sequence" concern that round-2 Q9 resolution targeted. Gather and Beacon emissions are NOT subject to either gate (they are point-in-time actions, not state-recurring pulses). This rule preserves Section B's traceability promise: standing still does not silently accumulate to Hunt-tier steady state. The Player Controller GDD owns the movement-delta detection logic AND the state-entry grace-window timer; the disturbance system accepts the magnitude it receives from the publisher.

**C.1.2 — Emission payload schema.**
Every emission carries the following fields, with no optional fields — all must be present or the emission is rejected by the server:

| Field | Type | Units / Range | Description |
|---|---|---|---|
| `emissionId` | string (UUID-format) | unique per emission | Globally unique identifier for traceability and death attribution. Generated via `HttpService:GenerateGUID(false)`; H.1 asserts the 8-4-4-4-12 hex format, not a specific UUID version |
| `sourcePlayerId` | UserId (int64) | valid connected player | Roblox UserId of the player whose action caused this emission |
| `emissionType` | EmissionType (string union) | {"Gather", "Sprint", "Light", "Beacon", "Craft"} | Categorical type used for predator weighting and HUD spike classification. Reserved enum slots for up to 3 future Crafting types per C.3.6 (1 of original 4 consumed 2026-04-30 by `Craft`) |
| `position` | Vector3 | world-space, studs | World position of the emitting action (node position for Gather; player position for Sprint/Light) |
| `initialMagnitude` | number | 0.0 – 1.0 (normalized) | Strength at age=0 before decay; values above 0.85 are reserved for Beacon |
| `emissionTime` | number | server clock, seconds | Server-authoritative timestamp at the moment of emission per D.1 clock spec (used to compute decay age) |
| `attributionChain` | {string} | length ≤ 4 | Ordered list of action labels recording what triggered this emission, for death-screen attribution; typically length 1. Length-cap is a runtime validation (not a Luau type constraint) |

**Published Luau types** (use these in `--!strict` modules; cite this section as canonical):

```luau
--!strict
-- All code blocks in this GDD assume --!strict mode per .claude/docs/technical-preferences.md.
-- Implementing modules MUST add --!strict at the top of the file.
type EmissionType = "Gather" | "Sprint" | "Light" | "Beacon" | "Craft"

type EmissionPayload = {
    emissionId: string,
    sourcePlayerId: number,
    emissionType: EmissionType,
    position: Vector3,
    initialMagnitude: number,
    emissionTime: number,
    attributionChain: {string},
}
```

**Cross-section type imports.** Modules implementing `EmissionPayload`, `DisturbanceAlertPayload` (C.3.4), or `HotspotResult` (C.3.3) must `require` the canonical type homes — `EmissionType` is canonical here in C.1.2; `Tier` is canonical in D.4; `AlertSubtype` is canonical in C.3.4. No GDD section may inline-redefine these unions.

**Module-scope `DisturbanceServiceType` alias (round-20 I-GP18-1 closure of round-18 visibility gap; cross-reference to F.2b row R17-I16-14 in the DisturbanceService ADR table; round-20 red-team R20-RT-5 corrected rationale).** Implementing modules that need to type-annotate a `local service: DisturbanceServiceType = self` pattern inside method bodies — typically C.1.11 step 5 (5-LC-a)'s per-respawn re-subscription helper at the `humanoid.Died:Connect` callback site, where the two-hop upvalue chain forces explicit type annotation per round-15 R14-B7 closure — MUST declare `type DisturbanceServiceType = typeof(DisturbanceService)` at **module scope** (top of `Init.luau` or equivalent), NOT inside individual method bodies. **Rationale (round-20 red-team R20-RT-5 closure of round-18 I-GP18-1 patch's overstated Knit-attached-methods claim).** The benefit of the module-scope alias is NOT that it captures more methods than `typeof(self)` inside a method body — under Luau's `--!strict` solver, `typeof(DisturbanceService)` declared at module scope evaluates to the service-table shape **as authored at the source-level** (before Knit's runtime metatable injection takes effect); a per-method-body `typeof(self)` could in some Knit-binding patterns infer the post-attach shape more precisely from the method body's own context. The actual benefit is that the module-scope alias provides a **stable named type anchor** for the two-hop upvalue chain closure problem (R14-B7 context): the closed-over `service` upvalue inside the `humanoid.Died:Connect` callback resolves through Luau's static analysis when annotated against a fixed named alias, where an inline `typeof(self)` would re-infer the type at each closure boundary (the inference is fragile across two upvalue hops and can resolve to `any`, silently disabling type-checking). The module-scope alias is the stable type-system anchor that ensures the closure's method-dispatch type-checks under `--!strict`; it is NOT a runtime-method-capture mechanism. The declaration is a `--!strict` compliance prerequisite for the round-15 R14-B7 closure to actually type-check end-to-end (a necessary, not sufficient condition — the implementer must still use the alias as the annotation type in the closure context). See F.2b row R17-I16-14 in the DisturbanceService ADR table (round-17 I16-14 introduction) for the full ADR-level commitment to the alias declaration site + the rationale chain.

**C.1.3 — Field value computation at a point (the rule).**
The disturbance field value at any world-space position P is a real number in [0.0, 1.0]. It is computed by summing the contributions of all live point sources within influence range of P, where each contribution is weighted by both the source's remaining magnitude (after decay) and an inverse-distance factor. **The field is 2D (XZ plane only)** — Y-coordinate is ignored in distance calculations. Caves and overlying terrain are not vertically separated by the field; this is acceptable because the art bible commits caves to one-squad-wide linear spaces (no branching), so vertical co-location is rare. The formula is in Section D. The rule: proximity amplifies contribution; older sources contribute less; multiple nearby sources stack.

**C.1.4 — Decay rule.**
Every point source loses magnitude over time from its `emissionTime` until it reaches zero, at which point it is marked expired. Decay curve in Section D. The rule: all emissions are temporary — no emission persists indefinitely. The server does not need to expire sources on a fixed interval; expiry can be computed lazily at query time by checking whether the current magnitude floor has been reached.

**C.1.5 — Spatial merge rule.**
There is no explicit merge operation. Multiple point sources that overlap in space each contribute independently to the field value at any queried position — the field value at P is the sum of all live contributions, clamped at 1.0. Sources do not cancel, do not fuse, and do not transfer magnitude. The perception of a "merged blob" is an emergent property of many overlapping contributions summing at query time.

**C.1.6 — Spatial index (REQUIRED for performance budget).**
The implementation MUST maintain a flat 2D grid (default cell size: 32 × 32 studs, in the XZ plane) over active emissions. Each emission is inserted into its containing cell on creation and removed on expiry. A query at position P reads only cells whose nearest point is within `INFLUENCE_RADIUS` of P. With default `SPATIAL_GRID_CELL_SIZE = 32` studs and `INFLUENCE_RADIUS = 24` studs, a query touches **up to 9 cells (a 3×3 neighborhood centered on P's cell)** — yielding O(k) cost per query where k = local emission count in those cells, not total live sources. **The spatial grid is a mandatory perf prerequisite for H.36's 2 ms/cycle budget — without it, the per-cycle cost grows as O(N × queryPositions) and exceeds the iPhone SE budget significantly.** The grid MUST also back `GetHottestHotspot` candidate enumeration in D.6, not only `fieldValue` queries — without this, hotspot search remains O(N) over all live sources at the predator's query cadence. Cell size is a tuning knob (G.3 `SPATIAL_GRID_CELL_SIZE`); the *existence* of the grid is contractual. The future `DisturbanceService` ADR specifies the data-structure choice (Roblox `Region3` vs. plain Luau table-of-tables); this GDD specifies only that one MUST exist.

**Clustered-source caveat.** When live sources cluster densely (e.g., 4 players sprinting and gathering in the same cell for 2+ minutes), the grid's per-query cost approaches O(N) because a single cell may hold the bulk of the source list. The `MAX_LIVE_SOURCES` cap (G.4) bounds the worst case; H.38b verifies the 2 ms cycle budget under clustered-source conditions.

**C.1.7 — Tier classification rule with hysteresis.**
At any *queried* position P, the tier is determined by the field value `t(P)` — one of four named tiers: Calm, Tense, Hunt, Retreat. (The field is not evaluated globally; a position's tier is meaningful only when a consumer queries it — round-16 I R16-I-12 clarity fix.) Tier boundaries (Section D): 0.30, 0.65, 1.00. **Hysteresis band: ±0.03 on every boundary** — a position transitions to a higher tier when `t` crosses the boundary upward; transitions back to a lower tier only when `t` falls below boundary − 0.03. This suppresses sub-second oscillation when `t` hovers near a boundary. Tier-at-position update cadence is the field-update cadence (C.1.11). A player's "current tier" is the tier of their position; the squad's "collective tier" is the highest tier among all connected players' positions.

**C.1.8 — Authority rule.**
The server is the sole canonical source of all disturbance data. Clients never compute, estimate, or hold authoritative copies of the field. **The world leads; the HUD follows.** Clients receive only derivative data pushed by the server: per-player `t` (for HUD meter), flora-saturation targets for nearby flora, and spike-alert events when an emission crosses a tier boundary. Clients may not query the field directly. Emission data lives as plain Luau tables inside the server-side `DisturbanceService` — **never** stored in Instance attributes or `Workspace` descendants, because StreamingEnabled would silently drop it.

**No client-callable emission path.** `DisturbanceService:Emit()` is a server-internal method. No `RemoteEvent`, `RemoteFunction`, `BindableEvent`, or `DisturbanceService.Client` method may trigger or proxy an `Emit()` call, directly or indirectly. Any future feature that conceptually requires "the client caused this emission" (e.g., a Crafting interaction) MUST route through a server-side authority that validates the action and then calls `Emit()` itself. Adding a client-facing emission path is an exploit-class regression — reviewers blocking PRs in this codebase MUST reject any change that violates this rule, and the `DisturbanceService` ADR will codify the enforcement check.

**C.1.9 — Visibility rule.**
Each client receives disturbance data scoped to its own player and its loaded chunks. A client does not receive the raw point-source list, other players' raw emissions, or the predator's field queries. The predator's field queries are server-internal and never transmitted to clients.

**C.1.10 — Cross-chunk emission persistence.**
Emissions persist server-side independently of any client's StreamingEnabled state. A chunk streaming out on the client does not expire or remove emissions in that chunk on the server — the server's field decay continues regardless of what any client renders. This is required for predator trail-following across chunk boundaries: an emission left behind by a player who has moved on still contributes to the field, and the predator can target it.

**C.1.11 — Traceability and death attribution rule.**

The server maintains **two separate emission stores**:

1. **Live-source list** — point sources with `m(t, age) > MAGNITUDE_FLOOR`. Used by all field-value math (D.1–D.6). Sources are removed on expiry per C.1.12.
2. **Attribution archive** — write-once-on-emission record keyed by `emissionId`, containing `{emissionId, sourcePlayerId, emissionType, position, emissionTime, attributionChain}`. Archive entries SURVIVE source expiry and are NOT used by any field-value math — they exist solely so death attribution can resolve `emissionId`s referenced by the predator's locked attribution list, even when the underlying source has decayed and been culled.

**Archive lifetime.** An archive entry is retained for the longer of: (a) `ATTRIBUTION_ARCHIVE_TTL` seconds from `emissionTime` (default 1200 s = 20 min, matching the upper bound of the concept doc's session-length window), OR (b) until all players who could reference it (the `sourcePlayerId` and any player who has died with this `emissionId` in their attribution chain) have left the server, OR (c) while the `emissionId` appears in any predator's currently-locked attribution list (Predator AI registers locked-on attribution back to the disturbance system via `DisturbanceService:RegisterPredatorLock(predatorId, {emissionId})` — server-internal, not exposed via Knit `Service.Client`; the registration is released either by Predator AI calling the corresponding `:ReleasePredatorLock(predatorId)` method, OR by `DisturbanceService` itself purging the registration on observed predator state-exit per the release semantics below). After the longest of those three conditions, the archive entry is purged. This bounds archive growth without losing attribution during a typical 5–20 min run AND without losing attribution for old emissions still actively driving predator behaviour (e.g., a 17-min-old gather emission the predator locked onto at minute 18 of a 20-minute session). **Count cap (round-23 B2/PF-1 — the archive previously had NO count bound analogous to `MAX_LIVE_SOURCES`):** the archive additionally enforces `MAX_ATTRIBUTION_ARCHIVE_ENTRIES` (G.4; default 4000). On overflow, evict-oldest by `emissionTime` (FIFO) with an ops-log entry — SKIPPING any entry currently referenced by a live predator-lock registration (condition (c) entries are eviction-exempt until their lock releases; eviction advances to the next-oldest unreferenced entry). Derivation: sustained natural play ≈ 3.1 emissions/s × the 1200 s TTL ≈ 3,720 worst-case natural accumulation — the 4000 default admits the natural worst case with ≈7% headroom (thin by design — round-24 Gate-B1 F1.5 note: raise within the safe range if playtest emission rates exceed the derivation's assumptions); the cap exists to bound pathological/exploit growth, not to trim natural play. Archive maintenance (TTL purge + cap enforcement) is a fenced, measured workload — H.36a workload 11.

**`RegisterPredatorLock` / `ReleasePredatorLock` release semantics (round-5 / Tier-1 #14; round-7a R7-B3 + R7-B7 patches applied).** `RegisterPredatorLock(predatorId, newList)` is **merge-on-re-register, not replace**: when a predator re-registers with a different list, the new list is **unioned** with the predator's prior list, then the union is pruned by archive-resolution (any `emissionId` that no longer resolves in the archive is silently dropped at union time — does NOT treat unresolvable IDs as retention holds, which would extend archive TTL forever for any expired ID a predator happened to lock). After union+prune, prior IDs that were not present in `newList` enter a **release-grace window** of `PREDATOR_LOCK_RELEASE_GRACE_WINDOW` seconds (G.4; default 5.0 s) during which they continue to satisfy retention condition (c). After the grace window expires they are removed from the registration set. **Rationale (round-7a R7-B7 fix):** the round-5 "replace-on-re-register" semantics created a data-loss window — if the predator re-registered at T=0.1 s with a refreshed top-3 (e.g., the squad pivoted to a new hotspot), the prior IDs were dropped from condition (c) immediately, and a player dying within the next ~5 s could receive a `DeathAttributionPayload` silently missing the prior contributors. The merge+grace pattern preserves attribution completeness across legitimate refresh cadence (Predator AI re-queries `GetHottestHotspot` at a fixed 4 Hz per G.6 / PA Core Rule 2 — round-22 I7 refresh — so re-registration intervals are ~0.25 s, well within the 5 s grace; the historical 1–4 Hz estimate's 1.0 s worst-case interval was also within grace, so the margin only improved) while still bounding archive growth (the grace window is small relative to `ATTRIBUTION_ARCHIVE_TTL = 1200 s`).

The complementary cleanup method, `DisturbanceService:ReleasePredatorLock(predatorId)`, is server-internal (never exposed via `DisturbanceService.Client`) and removes ALL entries the predator had locked, including those still inside the release-grace window. **Predator AI MUST call `:ReleasePredatorLock` on every state transition out of Hunt / Investigate / Disengage and on predator respawn / despawn.** As a defense-in-depth, `DisturbanceService` also listens for the predator's lifecycle on a **server-internal `OnPredatorStateChanged` BindableEvent** (owned by the Predator AI service per F.2a row 3 split-channel architecture; payload `{predatorId, fromState, toState, timestamp}`) and purges registrations on observed state-exit even if the Predator AI omitted the explicit `:ReleasePredatorLock` call. **Why a BindableEvent and NOT `OnPredatorLockChanged` (round-7a R7-B3 / CD adjudication D3 fix):** `OnPredatorLockChanged` is a client-facing Knit RemoteSignal — Knit fires RemoteSignals OVER THE NETWORK to clients, so a server-side subscriber on a Knit RemoteSignal cannot reliably receive the cross-service event (the previous round-5 wording assumed it could, which was a circular-dependency / silent-no-fire defect identified by 4-specialist convergence in round-7). The split-channel architecture authored in F.2a row 3 separates the cross-service server-internal BindableEvent (`OnPredatorStateChanged`) from the client-facing RemoteSignal (`OnPredatorLockChanged`); DisturbanceService subscribes to the former only. **The two cleanup paths are convergent — invoking both is safe (the second is a no-op on an already-empty registration set).** Verified by E.23 + H.30c.

**Predator-side recording.** The Predator AI's targeting system records, at the moment of target lock, the top-contributing `emissionId`s (up to 3, ordered by `contribution(bestPosition, S)` per D.6) that drove the lock. **Refresh on re-query.** When `GetHottestHotspot` returns a NEW `bestPosition` (one that differs from the predator's previously locked position by more than `HOTSPOT_DEDUP_GRID_SIZE` studs), the predator MUST replace its locked attribution with the new query's `topContributingEmissionIds`. This prevents stale attribution when the predator pivots from one player's hotspot to another mid-investigation.

**On player death**, the server reads the predator's currently-locked attribution list, resolves those `emissionId`s against the **attribution archive** (NOT the live-source list), and pushes a `DeathAttributionPayload` to the dead player's client. The payload populates the death screen: "You were found because of [action] by [player] [N seconds] ago at [map marker]." This rule is load-bearing for the Section B traceability commitment AND for the concept doc's MVP gate ("≥ 95% of deaths surface a legible cause").

**Cap-hit caveat.** When a `MAX_LIVE_SOURCES` cap drop occurs (E.12), no archive entry is written for the dropped emission — the dropped action did not contribute to any field state and cannot be resolved. The `DeathAttributionPayload` carries an `attributionMayBeIncomplete: boolean` flag set true if any cap drop occurred during the dead player's contribution window; the HUD displays a caveat in that case (see Q1 + HUD GDD). (Note: prose uses Luau primitive `boolean`, not `bool` — `bool` is not a Luau type.)

**Death-attribution ordering rule (round-7c B-7b-3 / R7-B7 grace-window coherence).** The 5 s `PREDATOR_LOCK_RELEASE_GRACE_WINDOW` (G.4) creates a window in which a predator has begun its state-out-of-Hunt-Investigate-Disengage transition AND its prior-locked emissionIds are still live in the attribution archive. A player death occurring inside this window admits two distinct death-screen meanings — "actively-hunted" vs "decayed-history" — and the HUD cannot disambiguate without a server-supplied bit. Implementations MUST observe the following strict ordering:
1. **Sample `predatorWasLockedAtDeath` first.** When `Humanoid.Died` fires for a player, server-side death handling MUST first resolve the predator-lock registry to compute `predatorWasLockedAtDeath` (true iff the dying player's UserId is the targeted-player of any live `RegisterPredatorLock` chain that resolves in the archive). This sampling is the FIRST mutation-relevant read of the registry in the death code path — it MUST NOT be sequenced after any predator-state-out-of-Hunt processing or release-grace-window expiry triggered by the death itself.
2. **Then construct `DeathAttributionPayload`** by resolving the predator's locked emissionIds against the archive (the archive read AFTER the lock-bit sample is by definition consistent with the sampled bit).
3. **Then trigger any death-as-state-trigger handling** that may transition the predator out of Hunt (e.g., predator de-locks because its target is dead). Predator AI state-machine transitions caused by the death MUST happen AFTER the payload is constructed.
4. **`OnPredatorLockChanged:Fire(targetedPlayer, {locked = false, ...})` is fired by `DisturbanceService:ReleasePredatorLock(predatorId)` itself — NOT by the Predator AI caller (round-9 DR-3 = Option B; supersedes the round-7c step-4 wording that placed fire-ownership on Predator AI).** Predator AI's responsibility on its own transition-out-of-Hunt/Investigate/Disengage (and DisturbanceService's responsibility on the BindableEvent purge fallback per E.23) is to invoke `:ReleasePredatorLock(predatorId)`; the method's body owns the fire ordering. The `:ReleasePredatorLock` body MUST execute the following internal sub-steps in this strict yield-free order, all within a single Luau frame:
   - **(4a)** Compute `pre_purge_targets` = the set of `targetedPlayer` UserIds whose entries this predator currently holds in the registry. The registry is the **single source of truth** for the targeted set; Predator AI MUST NOT shadow this set independently and MUST NOT pass it as an argument. No `GetLockedTargetedPlayers(predatorId)` public accessor is declared on the service — the registry remains encapsulated.
   - **(4b)** For each `targetedPlayer ∈ pre_purge_targets`, invoke `OnPredatorLockChanged:Fire(targetedPlayer, {locked = false, predatorId = predatorId})`. **Sync-callback prohibition (round-11 B-R10-1b closure).** No service or other server-side code path MAY attach a synchronous-side-effect observer to `OnPredatorLockChanged`'s server-side fire path that observes the for-loop iteration of (4b) before sub-step (4c) completes. Specifically forbidden: (i) direct `OnPredatorLockChanged.Event:Connect`-style server-side subscriptions on the underlying RemoteEvent's `.Event` signal (defensive prohibition; Knit RemoteSignals fire server-to-client over the network so the underlying RemoteEvent's `.Event` is the client-fired `OnServerEvent` path which is forbidden by F.4 raw-RemoteEvent server-fire-only rule for this signal anyway, but the round-11 B-R10-1b prohibition extends the rule to a defensive ban regardless of fire direction); (ii) BindableEvent fan-out helpers that mirror per-target fires from (4b) into a server-internal BindableEvent and accept synchronous subscribers; (iii) any other observation pattern that allows a service to receive synchronous notification of an in-progress (4b) iteration before sub-step (4c) atomically purges the registry. The prohibition closes the same-frame interleaving race where a synchronous subscriber on the fire path could observe partial-purge registry state between consecutive `:Fire` calls within the iteration (e.g., after `:Fire(targetedPlayer1, ...)` but before `:Fire(targetedPlayer2, ...)` and before sub-step (4c) purges both entries). Subscribers that need to react to predator-lock-clear events MUST do so via the standard client-side `OnPredatorLockChanged:Connect` (Knit RemoteSignal client subscription on the dead/affected player's HUD) — the server-side fire path is server-internal-only-during-the-(4b)-iteration even though `OnPredatorLockChanged` is a Knit RemoteSignal in steady state. The H.39e CI lint (round-11 addition; see Section H Security / Anti-Exploit Regression) operationalises this prohibition.
   - **(4c)** Atomically purge all entries for `predatorId` from the registry (including those still inside the release-grace window per C.1.11 archive lifetime condition (c)).

   Sub-steps (4a)–(4c) MUST execute with **no `task.wait`, no `task.spawn`, no `task.delay`, no `RemoteFunction:InvokeServer` / `:InvokeClient` round-trips, no `coroutine.yield`, and no other yield primitives that could surrender control to a Heartbeat boundary**. Roblox `RemoteSignal:Fire` and `BindableEvent:Fire` are explicitly permitted because they are non-yielding on the server (RemoteSignal:Fire dispatches asynchronously to clients without blocking the server thread; BindableEvent:Fire invokes connected callbacks synchronously, but each callback itself MUST also be yield-free for the duration of the (4a)–(4c) sequence — the test harness verifies this via H.30c sub-AC (e)'s instrumentation hooks). The yield-free guarantee makes the lock-bit-sample-vs-registry-purge ordering deterministic across Heartbeat boundaries and is the precondition that makes H.30c sub-AC (g) deterministically reproducible.

5. **`Humanoid.Died` subscription monopoly + DisturbanceService-owned `OnPlayerDied` BindableEvent dispatch (round-11 DR-4 = Option B; supersedes the round-9 KnitInit-vs-KnitStart subscription-ownership rule).** The round-9 rule rested ordering on Roblox's `:Connect` registration-order semantics on `Humanoid.Died` — a guarantee that is NOT formally documented in Roblox's published API contracts. Round-10 ai-programmer + game-designer + ai-programmer convergent finding (3-spec cluster) identified this as a load-bearing assumption on a HIGH-risk auto-updating live platform: a Roblox-side change to `:Connect` ordering would silently break C.1.11 step 1's lock-bit-sample-first guarantee AND the H.30c sub-AC (h) 100-trial harness, with no compile-time signal. Round-11 DR-4 = Option B replaces the ordering-by-registration-order architecture with a BindableEvent dispatch architecture in which DisturbanceService is the **sole subscriber** to `Humanoid.Died` AND owns dispatch ordering programmatically.

   **DisturbanceService MUST be the only code path in `src/server/**/*.luau` (and indeed anywhere in the server codebase) with a `Humanoid.Died:Connect` chain.** All other services that previously subscribed to `Humanoid.Died` directly (Player Controller, HUD, Predator AI, audio system, etc.) MUST subscribe to DisturbanceService's server-internal `OnPlayerDied` BindableEvent instead.

   DisturbanceService declares a server-internal `OnPlayerDied` BindableEvent during `KnitInit`; the BindableEvent is NOT exposed via `DisturbanceService.Client` and is NOT replicated to clients (it is a server-internal cross-service event channel). The payload type is declared as a published Luau type alias for `--!strict` compliance across subscriber services (round-11 BLOCK-3 / red-team I-3 closure):

   ```luau
   type OnPlayerDiedPayload = {
       deadPlayer: Player,
       deathTimestamp: number,        -- workspace:GetServerTimeNow() at the moment of death
       predatorWasLockedAtDeath: boolean,  -- captured atomically at sub-step (5a) per C.1.11 step 5
   }
   ```

   Subscriber services acquire the BindableEvent reference during their own `KnitStart` via a server-internal accessor `DisturbanceService:GetOnPlayerDiedSignal(): BindableEvent` (concrete signature is implementation-not-contract; the binding requirement is only that the BindableEvent is reachable from other services' `KnitStart` code path, that the accessor itself is server-internal-only — H.39c verifies non-Client declaration — and that the accessor's return value is the same BindableEvent instance across all calls within a server session, not per-call freshly-constructed). Subscriber callbacks type-annotate the payload as `OnPlayerDiedPayload` (e.g., `signal:Connect(function(payload: OnPlayerDiedPayload) ... end)`) — the type alias is exported via DisturbanceService's public Luau type-export module.

   **Subscriber import contract for `OnPlayerDiedPayload` (round-20 GP19-1 closure of round-19 gameplay-programmer finding — explicit import path).** Subscriber modules MUST acquire the type alias via the canonical-type-export module pattern: `local DisturbanceTypes = require(<DisturbanceService type-export module path>)` followed by `type OnPlayerDiedPayload = DisturbanceTypes.OnPlayerDiedPayload` declared at module scope. The concrete type-export module path is owned by the DisturbanceService ADR (recorded as F.2b row R17-I16-14's companion ADR entry covering the BindableEvent-payload export site; the path itself is implementation-not-contract — typical candidates are `src/server/services/DisturbanceService/Types.luau` exporting a single types-table, or `src/server/services/DisturbanceService/Init.luau` re-exporting types alongside the service constructor). Subscribers MUST NOT redeclare the `OnPlayerDiedPayload` type locally — local redeclaration is a regression class that drifts whenever this GDD adds payload fields (e.g., a future `victim_lastEmissionId: string?` field for diagnostic UI; e.g., a future `pre_segment_profile: string` field aligned with H.PB1-BETA's pre-segment activity rubric). The single-canonical-export rule + Luau `--!strict` type-identity discipline ensures payload drift fails-loud at compile time across all subscriber modules (Predator AI, HUD, Player Controller, Crafting). The DisturbanceService ADR's first revision MUST publish the concrete type-export module path; until the ADR lands, subscriber GDD authors MAY cite this contract as the binding import surface AND treat the path-pinning as a forward obligation tracked under the same F.2b R17-I16-14 ADR row.

   DisturbanceService's `Humanoid.Died` callback (registered exclusively during `DisturbanceService:KnitInit` via the standard `Players.PlayerAdded` → `Player.CharacterAdded` → `humanoid.Died` subscription chain) executes the following strict yield-free sequence within a single Luau frame:
   - **(5a)** `self:_captureDeathLockSnapshot(deadPlayer)` — idempotent server-internal helper that reads the lock registry's state for `deadPlayer.UserId` and stores `predatorWasLockedAtDeath: boolean` into `self._deathLockSnapshot[deadPlayer]`. The snapshot is the FIRST mutation-relevant read of the registry in the death code path. The corresponding `Players.PlayerRemoving` cleanup clears `self._deathLockSnapshot[deadPlayer]` (the R9-I2 retention semantics ADR obligation specifies a session-bounded TTL fallback for the rare case where session terminates after `Humanoid.Died` fires but before `:GetDeathAttributionPayload` is invoked).
   - **(5b)** Construct the BindableEvent payload `{deadPlayer = deadPlayer, deathTimestamp = workspace:GetServerTimeNow(), predatorWasLockedAtDeath = self._deathLockSnapshot[deadPlayer]}`. The payload is constructed AFTER the snapshot capture so the flag value is consistent with the registry read at sub-step (5a).
   - **(5c)** `self._OnPlayerDied:Fire(payload)` — fan out synchronously to all subscribed services in DisturbanceService-controlled order. Subscribers receive the payload synchronously per Roblox `BindableEvent:Fire` semantics; their connected callbacks MUST themselves be yield-free across the (5a)–(5c) window per the same yield-free contract that applies to sub-steps (4a)–(4c). Specifically forbidden inside any `OnPlayerDied:Connect`-registered callback body: `task.wait`, `task.spawn`, `task.delay`, `coroutine.yield`, `:InvokeServer`, `:InvokeClient`, and any other Roblox primitive that surrenders control to a Heartbeat boundary — **with no exception**. Callbacks that need to do async work MUST use the **deferred-queue pattern** specified by H.39e Lint (b): the synchronous callback body stashes the payload onto a service-internal per-frame queue (e.g., `table.insert(self._pendingDeaths, payload)`), AND a separate `RunService.Heartbeat:Connect` handler — registered in the SAME service's `KnitStart`, **NOT** inside the `OnPlayerDied:Connect` callback — pops queue entries and processes them asynchronously OUTSIDE the `OnPlayerDied:Connect` callback's lexical scope. The queue-stash + Heartbeat-worker decoupling preserves the synchronous receipt phase's yield-free guarantee (the callback body itself contains zero yield primitives — H.39e grep-only lint) while admitting async post-processing on a subsequent frame. **The round-11 wording that permitted "`task.spawn(function() ... end)` an outer wrapper" inside the callback is REMOVED in round-13** — H.39e Lint (b) is grep-only with no exceptions, and the deferred-queue pattern is the sole sanctioned async-work mechanism for `OnPlayerDied:Connect` subscribers. See H.39e for the reference implementation pattern (informational; not normative beyond the grep-detectable absence of yield primitives in the callback body).

   **Subscription / snapshot lifecycle rules (round-13 R12-B6 promotion of R11-I7 to normative text — these rules are load-bearing for the implementer; the round-11 ADR-deferral was incorrect because the subscription chain cannot be written without them):**
   - **(5-LC-a) Per-respawn re-subscription.** DisturbanceService MUST re-register the `humanoid.Died:Connect` chain on **every** `Player.CharacterAdded` fire for that player, including respawns within the same session. The canonical idiom (registered exclusively in `DisturbanceService:KnitInit`) MUST contain **exactly one `humanoid.Died:Connect(...)` call site** to satisfy H.39e Lint (a)'s `== 1` cardinality requirement; the per-respawn re-registration is achieved by routing both the new-CharacterAdded path AND the race-window-safety path through a SINGLE local helper that contains the only `:Connect` site:

     ```luau
     -- Inside DisturbanceService:KnitInit() body — the local helper is lexically
     -- nested inside KnitInit and contains the only humanoid.Died:Connect call site
     -- in the entire server codebase. Both call paths (CharacterAdded and the
     -- race-window-safety branch) invoke the helper rather than reproducing the
     -- :Connect call. H.39e Lint (a) returns exactly one match, lexically nested
     -- inside KnitInit's body — PASS.
     local service: typeof(DisturbanceService) = self  -- round-15 R14-B7 closure:
         -- explicit type annotation on closure capture so the inner :_onHumanoidDied
         -- invocation type-checks under --!strict. Without this, Knit's service-table
         -- inference can resolve `self` to `any` across the two-hop upvalue chain
         -- (KnitInit's `self` → registerDeathHandler's enclosing scope → the :Connect
         -- callback closure), silently disabling type-checking on the method dispatch.
     local function registerDeathHandler(humanoid: Humanoid, player: Player)
         humanoid.Died:Connect(function() service:_onHumanoidDied(player) end)
     end
     Players.PlayerAdded:Connect(function(player)
         player.CharacterAdded:Connect(function(character)
             local humanoid = character:WaitForChild("Humanoid") :: Humanoid
             registerDeathHandler(humanoid, player)
         end)
         if player.Character then
             -- Race-window safety: CharacterAdded may have already fired before
             -- this Players.PlayerAdded handler ran for a player who joined earlier.
             -- The same helper's :Connect call applies — exactly one source-level
             -- :Connect site is invoked twice, NOT two distinct :Connect sites.
             local humanoid = player.Character:WaitForChild("Humanoid") :: Humanoid
             registerDeathHandler(humanoid, player)
         end
     end)
     ```

     **Single-call-site discipline (round-13 R12-B6 / red-team B1 closure).** The two-`:Connect`-call-sites idiom that round-13's first-pass authored (one inside the CharacterAdded callback, one inside the race-window safety branch) was red-team-flagged as breaking H.39e Lint (a)'s grep-cardinality predicate (the lint returns ALL matches across the codebase regardless of lexical nesting; two source-level call sites = two matches = `count != 1` = FAIL). The single-helper refactor closes the defect: exactly one `humanoid.Died:Connect(...)` source-level site exists; the helper is invoked from both call paths but the `:Connect` declaration is authored ONCE. **Single-fire-per-death runtime guarantee.** The helper produces a fresh `RBXScriptConnection` on each invocation. For a given Humanoid instance, the helper is invoked at most once per Character lifecycle (either via the new-CharacterAdded path OR the race-window safety branch — never both for the same Humanoid, because the race-window branch only fires when `player.Character` exists at PlayerAdded time, and CharacterAdded does NOT re-fire for an already-loaded Character). Across a player's session, the helper is invoked once per Character lifecycle (one initial spawn + N respawns = N+1 invocations against N+1 distinct Humanoid instances). Old connections on prior `Humanoid` instances are garbage-collected when the previous Character despawns. The H.39e Lint (a) static-cardinality check is satisfied; the runtime single-fire-per-death contract is preserved. **Round-15 R14-B7 closure (closure-self type annotation under `--!strict`).** The captured `self` upvalue inside the `humanoid.Died:Connect`-registered callback crosses two function boundaries — the outer `KnitInit` method's `self` flows into `registerDeathHandler`'s enclosing scope, then again into the `:Connect` callback closure. Knit's service-table type inference does not reliably propagate the service type through this two-hop upvalue chain under `--!strict`; the captured value can resolve to `any`, silently disabling type-checking on the `:_onHumanoidDied(player)` method dispatch. The fix is the explicit `local service: typeof(DisturbanceService) = self` capture shown in the code block above; the helper and its inner callback close over the explicitly-typed local instead of the outer `self` upvalue. Runtime semantics are unchanged (the local references the same table); the type-checker now has an anchor for the two-hop upvalue resolution. Implementations MUST use this pattern (or an equivalent that is provably typed under `--!strict`) — anchoring `self` once at the helper's enclosing scope is the canonical idiom for any future `:Connect`-registered callback inside `DisturbanceService:KnitInit` that invokes a service method.
   - **(5-LC-b) Second-death overwrite semantics.** When the same player dies twice in a session (e.g., respawned after first death, then died again), the second death's sub-step (5a) `_captureDeathLockSnapshot(deadPlayer)` invocation **overwrites** any prior `_deathLockSnapshot[deadPlayer]` entry that was not yet read. Each death's payload reflects ITS OWN snapshot — there is no merging, no first-death-wins precedence, and no second-death rejection. The implementer MUST treat `_deathLockSnapshot[deadPlayer]` as a single-cell mailbox: the latest death's snapshot is always the live value; readers receive the snapshot from the most recent death-as-of-read. (The first death's snapshot is reachable to a reader ONLY in the brief in-frame window between sub-step (5a) of death #1 and the corresponding `:GetDeathAttributionPayload` invocation that consumes it; if the consumer races against death #2's (5a), it observes death #2's snapshot — correct under R11-I7's "each death's payload reflects its own snapshot" rule.)
   - **(5-LC-c) Read-and-clear atomicity on `:GetDeathAttributionPayload`.** The `DisturbanceService:GetDeathAttributionPayload(deadPlayer): DeathAttributionPayload` call MUST read `_deathLockSnapshot[deadPlayer]` and clear the entry **atomically within a single Luau frame** (no yield between the read and the clear). The canonical idiom: `local snapshot = self._deathLockSnapshot[deadPlayer]; self._deathLockSnapshot[deadPlayer] = nil; return constructPayload(snapshot, ...)`. The H.39e Lint (b) yield-primitive ban applies to the read-and-clear sequence's lexical scope: between the table-read and the `nil`-assignment there MUST be zero yield primitives (no `task.wait`, no `task.spawn`, no `:InvokeServer`, etc.). The cleared-entry semantics protects against re-resolution of a stale snapshot if the consumer requests the payload twice (the second call sees `nil` and follows the E.21 silent-no-op path).
   - **(5-LC-d) Single-writer invariant.** Only the `Humanoid.Died` callback registered in `DisturbanceService:KnitInit` (per (5-LC-a)) writes `_deathLockSnapshot[player]`. No other code path — not `Players.PlayerRemoving`, not `:GetDeathAttributionPayload`, not Predator AI's `OnPlayerDied:Connect` callback, not any test-only seam — MAY write to `_deathLockSnapshot[player]` with a non-nil value. Permitted writers of `nil` (clears): the (5-LC-c) read-and-clear path AND the `Players.PlayerRemoving` cleanup AND the optional R9-I2 session-bounded TTL fallback (DisturbanceService ADR-owned). The single-writer invariant is the structural closure of the multi-writer race that R11-I7 (b) flagged.

   **Ordering contract (round-11 DR-4 = Option B structural closure of B-CC1-B).** Because DisturbanceService is the sole `Humanoid.Died` subscriber, the death-lock snapshot is captured BEFORE any other service learns about the death; any registry-mutating cleanup any other service would have triggered (Predator AI's transition-out-of-Hunt, HUD's death-screen construction) now happens in response to `OnPlayerDied:Fire`, by which time `_deathLockSnapshot[deadPlayer]` is already populated and immutable. The connection-order race that round-9 closed via `KnitInit`-vs-`KnitStart` ordering is now closed structurally — there is exactly one `Humanoid.Died` subscriber in the entire server codebase, so registration-order is irrelevant, and the load-bearing reliance on Roblox's undocumented `:Connect` ordering semantics is eliminated. Predator AI's `OnPlayerDied:Connect` callback MAY invoke `:ReleasePredatorLock(predatorId)` from within its body if the death-as-trigger should release a predator lock; this triggers the sub-steps (4a)–(4c) yield-free purge per step 4. Subsequent `:GetDeathAttributionPayload(deadPlayer)` invocations read the immutable `_deathLockSnapshot` for the flag value, NOT the live registry — so the payload's flag reflects the registry state at the moment of death regardless of any later `:ReleasePredatorLock` invocation triggered by `OnPlayerDied` subscribers.

   **CI lint enforcement (round-11 DR-5 = Option A — operationalised by H.39e).** A grep over `src/server/**/*.luau` MUST reject any `Humanoid.Died:Connect` (or `humanoid.Died:Connect` / any `:Connect` chained on a `humanoid.Died` reference) call appearing anywhere in the server codebase other than inside `DisturbanceService:KnitInit(`'s body. The same lint also forbids any synchronous yield primitive (`task.wait`, `task.spawn`, `task.delay`, `coroutine.yield`, `:InvokeServer`, `:InvokeClient`) inside any `OnPlayerDied:Connect`-registered callback body, AND forbids any synchronous-side-effect observation on `OnPredatorLockChanged`'s server-side fire path (round-11 B-R10-1b — closes the same-frame synchronous-callback observation race on the sub-step (4b) per-target fire loop; see step 4 sub-step (4b) sync-callback prohibition). The H.39e CI gate (round-11 addition; see Section H Security / Anti-Exploit Regression) operationalises all three lints. Integration-level verification is H.30c sub-AC (h) (round-11-updated to verify single-subscriber dispatch via the `OnPlayerDied` BindableEvent rather than registration-order semantics on `Humanoid.Died`).

**Why this architecture closes the five Cluster-1 races identified by round-8 (B-CC1-A through B-CC1-E) — round-11 DR-4 = Option B update:**
- B-CC1-A is closed because no `GetLockedTargetedPlayers` accessor is declared — sub-step (4a) reads the registry from inside DisturbanceService itself, preserving Knit encapsulation.
- B-CC1-B is closed **structurally** by step 5's `Humanoid.Died` subscription monopoly — there is exactly one subscriber in the entire server codebase (DisturbanceService), so Roblox's `:Connect` registration-order semantics on `Humanoid.Died` are no longer load-bearing on a documented-or-undocumented platform guarantee. The H.39e CI lint mechanically enforces the monopoly. (Round-9's KnitInit-vs-KnitStart ordering rule was a workaround that traded an undocumented Roblox API guarantee for a Knit-API guarantee; round-11 DR-4 = Option B eliminates the dependency on either.)
- B-CC1-C is closed because the `_deathLockSnapshot` is captured atomically on the sole `Humanoid.Died` fire (sub-step 5a) and is immutable for the lifetime of `_deathLockSnapshot[deadPlayer]`; concurrent `task.spawn`-driven re-queries from Predator AI cannot mutate the per-death snapshot AND cannot interleave with the snapshot capture because the (5a)–(5c) sequence is yield-free.
- B-CC1-D is closed because the `OnPlayerDied` BindableEvent dispatch at sub-step (5c) is owned by DisturbanceService's own code path; subscriber callbacks invoke `:ReleasePredatorLock(predatorId)` if needed, which executes sub-steps (4a)–(4c) under the same yield-free contract — the BindableEvent inbound side has no registry-mutating responsibility outside DisturbanceService's own method body. The E.23 BindableEvent purge fallback path (Predator AI's `OnPredatorStateChanged` BindableEvent fan-out, owned by Predator AI for non-death state transitions) likewise routes through `:ReleasePredatorLock` for any registry mutation.
- B-CC1-E is closed because sub-steps (4a)–(4c)'s yield-free guarantee is now codified as a normative MUST AND extended to (5a)–(5c) AND mechanically enforced by the H.39e CI lint's yield-primitive ban inside `OnPlayerDied:Connect` callback bodies AND on the `OnPredatorLockChanged` server-side fire path (sub-step (4b) sync-callback prohibition).

Step 1's lock-bit-sample-first guarantees the death-screen attribution reflects the registry state at the moment of death (not at the moment of payload construction). Step 4's during-registry-purge fire ordering at sub-step (4b) guarantees the HUD's predator-locked indicator and the death-screen's `predatorWasLockedAtDeath` flag agree. Step 5's BindableEvent-monopoly subscription guarantees the snapshot is captured before any other service can mutate the registry — the structural closure of the connection-order race that round-9 closed only by API-guarantee assumption. Without all three rules, the 5 s grace window admits a death-screen "actively-hunted" framing for a predator the HUD already said disengaged — squad-trust read of "we know exactly when the predator gave up" only holds if these surfaces are causally ordered. Verified by H.30c (round-11 update — sub-AC (e) updated to verify (5a)/(5b)/(5c) timestamps in the new BindableEvent dispatch; sub-AC (h) updated to verify the BindableEvent monopoly via H.39e lint instead of the round-9 KnitInit-vs-KnitStart connection-order determinism; sub-AC (i) concurrent-task.spawn invariant unchanged).

**C.1.12 — Lifecycle rule.**
A point source is live from its `emissionTime` until its decayed magnitude falls **strictly below** `MAGNITUDE_FLOOR` (Section G) — i.e., expiry condition is `m(age) < MAGNITUDE_FLOOR`, not `≤`. This guarantees that a source created with `initialMagnitude = MAGNITUDE_FLOOR` survives at least one query pass at age=0 (see E.2 / H.10). Expiry evaluation is deferred to the maintenance pass, not performed at insertion or mid-cycle: a source inserted during a field-update cycle is always queryable for the remainder of that cycle. The server culls expired sources on a low-frequency maintenance pass (target cadence: once per 2–4 seconds, not every Heartbeat). The hard cap `MAX_LIVE_SOURCES` (G.4) bounds list growth between maintenance passes; see E.12 for cap-hit behaviour.

**C.1.13 — Update cadence rule.**
The field is **not** recomputed every server Heartbeat tick. The server runs the disturbance update loop at **5 Hz** (default; tunable in Section G to 2–10 Hz). The driver is `RunService.Heartbeat` with a manual time-accumulator (`task.wait` loops are not used — they drift under server load). Flora saturation targets are pushed to clients when a flora's local `t` changes by more than `FLORA_UPDATE_DELTA_THRESHOLD` (Section G). This decoupling from Heartbeat is required by the iPhone SE-class performance floor and the 50 KB/s per-client bandwidth ceiling.

**Heartbeat catch-up semantics (REQUIRED).** When `RunService.Heartbeat` fires with `dt` greater than the update interval (server hitch — e.g., dt = 0.5s while interval = 0.2s), the accumulator pattern fires the update pass **at most once per Heartbeat callback**, then resets remaining accumulated debt to zero. Catch-up loops (firing twice or three times in a single Heartbeat to consume debt) are FORBIDDEN — they amplify the hitch by adding work to a frame already over budget. This is graceful skip, not graceful catch-up. The visible consequence during a hitch is one missed update interval; field decay remains correct because `m(age)` reads wall-clock `serverTime - emissionTime` regardless of how many update passes ran (D.1).

**Per-source decay caching (REQUIRED).** Every update pass MUST evaluate `m(age)` exactly **once per live source**, cache the value on the source record, and reuse the cached value for every field-value query within that same pass (flora positions, player positions, hotspot candidates). Calling `math.exp()` per-source-per-query-position is forbidden — without caching, the per-cycle cost grows as O(N × queryPositions) and exceeds the iPhone SE 2 ms budget significantly. The actual query-position count at H.36 test conditions is approximately `(MAX_FLORA_PER_CHUNK × loaded_chunks) + connectedPlayers ≈ 8 × 2 + 4 = 20` query positions per cycle (anchored to art-bible §6.5 max flora density and H.36 "two loaded chunks"). Even at 20 query positions × 200 live sources, uncached exp would consume substantial budget. This rule is verified by H.9b. Once a source's decayed magnitude falls below `MAGNITUDE_FLOOR`, subsequent passes skip the `exp()` call entirely and mark the source for the next maintenance-pass cull.

**Forbidden non-exp transcendentals.** Within the inner contribution loop (D.2 falloff calc), `math.pow` is forbidden — use `(1 - d/r) * (1 - d/r)` for squaring. **The Luau `^` numeric operator is also forbidden in the inner contribution loop** (round-4 addition): on some Luau VM builds `a^b` compiles to a transcendental call equivalent to `exp(b * log(a))` even when `b == 2`, which defeats the no-pow rule and is invisible to a `math.pow` counter. Use explicit multiplication (`x * x`) for squaring; `math.sqrt` is forbidden in the radius cull check — compare squared distances per `dist2DSquared` (D.2). H.9c verifies all three constraints alongside H.9b.

### States and Transitions

The disturbance field itself is continuous; there is no discrete state machine governing it. The four named **tiers** define the state of *any world position* at any moment, and downstream systems (predator behavior, flora visuals, HUD) change behavior based on the tier of a position.

| Tier | `t` range | Flora visual state | Predator disposition | HUD state | Transition rule |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Calm** | 0.00 – 0.299 | Dull biolume; pulse 8s; Brightness Lerp at 0.3 base | Passive patrol; no directional bias toward this position | Meter low; no spike alerts active | Enters from Tense when `t` drops < 0.27 (Tense hysteresis −0.03). Initial state on first classification when `t` < 0.30 (D.4 first-call flat dispatch). |
| **Tense** | 0.30 – 0.649 | Moderate saturation; pulse 4–5s; Brightness mid | Alerted; predator may orient toward this hotspot if no stronger hotspot exists | Meter mid; tier-crossing spike alert fires once on entry | Enters from Calm when `t` ≥ 0.30 (no hysteresis on upward). Enters from Hunt when `t` < 0.62 (Hunt hysteresis −0.03). Initial state on first classification when 0.30 ≤ `t` < 0.65. Exits to Calm when `t` < 0.27. |
| **Hunt** | 0.65 – 0.999 | High saturation; pulse 1.5–2s; Brightness near 2.0 | Actively investigating; predator locomotion speed up | Meter high; spike alert; screen-edge ping if predator orients | Enters from Tense when `t` ≥ 0.65 (no hysteresis on upward). Enters from Retreat when `t` < 0.97 (Retreat hysteresis −0.03). Initial state on first classification when 0.65 ≤ `t` < 1.00. Exits to Tense when `t` < 0.62. |
| **Retreat** | 1.00 (clamped) | Full saturation; pulse 0.3–0.5s; Brightness clamped 2.0 | Maximum aggression; predator commits to attack run | Meter pinned max; alert sustained | Enters from Hunt when clamped contributions reach 1.0. Initial state on first classification when `t` ≥ 1.00. Exits to Hunt when `t` < 0.97. |

**Retreat is a ceiling, not a stable state.** Because emissions decay, a position cannot remain at t=1.0 indefinitely. Retreat is entered when clamped contributions sum ≥ 1.0 and exited as decay reduces the sum below 1.0 (less hysteresis). Always transient.

**Per-player vs. per-position tiers.** Players move through the field. A player's "current tier" updates as their position's field value changes — they can walk out of a Tense zone into a Calm zone and their personal tier drops accordingly. The predator queries the field independently of player positions: it finds the hottest hotspot, which may not be where any player currently stands (e.g., a freshly-gathered node cluster the squad has already moved away from).

### Interactions with Other Systems

#### C.3.1 — Resource Node → Disturbance System (publisher)

The Resource Node system publishes a `GatherEmission` at the moment a gather action *completes* (not starts). The Resource Node owns the gather lifecycle; the disturbance system has no knowledge of gather progress, only completion.

- Resource Node calls (server-internal direct method): `DisturbanceService:Emit(emissionType="Gather", position=nodeWorldPosition, initialMagnitude=<node-defined>, sourcePlayerId=actingPlayerId)`
- Magnitude per node type configured in Resource Node's data table (not here). Disturbance system accepts what it receives.
- **Guarantee**: emission recorded server-side before the gather reward is granted. If the emit call fails (server error), the gather is still granted — disturbance failure is non-blocking for gameplay.
- No acknowledgment returned; fire-and-forget.

#### C.3.2 — Player Controller → Disturbance System (publisher)

Player Controller publishes two emission types: `SprintEmission` (on sprint-state entry and every 2s thereafter while sprinting) and `LightEmission` (on lantern raise and every 4s while above ambient brightness threshold).

- Player Controller calls: `DisturbanceService:Emit(emissionType="Sprint"|"Light", position=playerWorldPosition, initialMagnitude=<constant per type>, sourcePlayerId=playerId)`
- Sprint and Light magnitudes are constants in Section G (Tuning Knobs).
- Player Controller is responsible for detecting state transitions and the interval timer; the disturbance system is stateless w.r.t. player movement state.
- Same fire-and-forget contract as C.3.1.

#### C.3.3 — Disturbance System → Predator AI (subscriber + query)

Two interaction modes: passive notification + active query.

**Notification**:
- When any emission's *cumulative* contribution pushes a position's `t` above a tier threshold (Calm→Tense, Tense→Hunt, Hunt→Retreat), the disturbance system fires a server-internal `TierCrossedEvent` carrying `{crossedTier, position, emissionId, initialMagnitude}`.
- Predator AI subscribes via Knit signal pattern (server-internal, zero-network) and uses it to update its threat-awareness state machine.
- **TierCrossedEvent is activation-signal-only.** The `position` field is informational (under round-3 coalescing it is the highest-cumulative position among same-pass crossings of the same boundary; outside coalescing it is the triggering emission's position). It is NOT authoritative for navigation. Predator AI MUST NOT navigate to `TierCrossedEvent.position`; it MUST always issue a fresh `GetHottestHotspot` call to obtain a navigation target. This rule is non-negotiable: violating it causes the predator to chase potentially-stale or non-peak positions and produces unintended targeting behaviour.
- TierCrossedEvent firing is gated by D.4 hysteresis (it derives from the same tier-classification pass), so it does not chatter when `t` oscillates within a hysteresis band.
- **Nil-recovery posture (REQUIRED).** When a `TierCrossedEvent` arrives but the subsequent `GetHottestHotspot` call returns nil (e.g., the triggering emission decayed or was deduplicated below `minimumTier` between the event firing and the predator's query), the Predator AI MUST treat this as a stale signal and remain in its current state (do NOT transition toward Investigate / Hunt on the strength of the event alone). The disturbance contract is responsible for the activation signal; the freshness-of-targets is the query's authority. This rule prevents the predator from chasing positions that no longer carry hostile field state.
- **Server-side per-pass coalescing (REQUIRED — round-3 ruling).** Within a single field-update pass, multiple positions may cross the same tier boundary. The disturbance system MUST coalesce these server-side: **at most one `TierCrossedEvent` fires per field-update pass per `crossedTier` boundary value.** When multiple positions cross the same boundary in the same pass, the system selects the position carrying the highest cumulative `fieldValue` at pass-end as the event's `position` field, and uses that triggering position's most-contributing `emissionId` for the event's `emissionId` field. Subscribers receive at most one event per pass per boundary and need no debouncing logic. This decision moves the synchronization burden off the Predator AI and into this system, where pass boundaries are observable. H.28 verifies single-event-per-pass-per-boundary.
- **Activation pipeline (numbered sequence — Predator AI obligation).** When a TierCrossedEvent arrives, the Predator AI MUST follow this ordered sequence on each predator tick: (1) note the dirty-flag set by event receipt; (2) do NOT use `event.position` for navigation (TierCrossedEvent.position is informational, not authoritative — see below); (3) call `GetHottestHotspot(callerPosition, searchRadius, minimumTier)` supplying its own current world-space position as `callerPosition` (round-17 I16-11 — F.1 preservation); (4) if the call returns nil, apply the nil-recovery rule and remain in current state; (5) if non-nil, evaluate path-stability delta against the previously navigated-to position — **first-call bootstrap: if no previously-navigated-to position exists (server start, post-Patrol→Investigate transition with no prior nav target, post-Retreat exit with cleared nav target), the predator MUST treat delta as ∞ (unconditional) and proceed to step 6 to invoke pathfinding on this first activation; the Predator AI GDD is responsible for clearing the previously-navigated-to position on Investigate→Patrol fallback per F.2a row 1, and the disturbance contract assumes this clear**; (6) if `delta >= PREDATOR_PATH_STABILITY_MIN_DELTA`, re-invoke `PathfindingService:FindPathAsync()`; otherwise, continue current navigation; (7) refresh locked attribution if `topContributingEmissionIds` differs (set-equality, order-insensitive — see attribution refresh rule below). This sequence is normative for Predator AI consumers of this contract.
- **Signal-to-state mapping (minimum spec).** The disturbance contract defines the activation signals; the Predator AI GDD owns the full state machine. **Naming-collision note (round-5 correction):** the disturbance tier `"Retreat"` (`t = 1.0` clamped, per D.4) and the predator's `Disengage` behavioural state are distinct concepts. The concept doc round-2 renamed the predator's pull-back state from "Retreat" to "Disengage" precisely to eliminate this collision. Throughout this section, `"Retreat"` (in code, in Luau type literals, and in tier-classification prose) refers ONLY to the disturbance tier; the predator's pull-back behaviour is referred to as `Disengage (predator state)`. The Predator AI GDD owns the full naming.

  Minimum mapping the predator MUST honour: `TierCrossedEvent(crossedTier="Tense")` is the wake signal for any Patrol→Investigate transition; the transition Investigate→Hunt is driven by `GetHottestHotspot` returning `fieldValue ≥ HUNT_THRESHOLD`, not by `TierCrossedEvent` alone (because TierCrossedEvent's position is not authoritative — see above). **Disengage (predator state)** is driven by `GetSquadAggregateT(excludeLockedPlayer)` (the round-23 B1 exclusion form — see Query below; round-24 Gate-A fix of the sixth stale zero-arg site) staying below `RETREAT_DROPOUT_THRESHOLD` for `RETREAT_SUSTAIN_DURATION` seconds; it is NOT driven by per-position field values. (`RETREAT_DROPOUT_THRESHOLD` and `RETREAT_SUSTAIN_DURATION` are the historical names of these knobs in G.6 and stay that way for cross-doc continuity; the predator state they drive is `Disengage`, not the disturbance tier `Retreat`.)

**Query**:
- Predator AI calls `DisturbanceService:GetHottestHotspot(callerPosition, searchRadius, minimumTier)` — returns the contract below — the world-space position with the highest local field value within `searchRadius` of `callerPosition` that is at or above `minimumTier` (round-17 I16-11: `callerPosition` is the search-center supplied by the caller, NOT pulled via Knit.GetService — preserves F.1 no-MVP-internal-hard-dependencies).
- Predator drives its own query cadence — the APPROVED Predator AI GDD commits to a **fixed 4 Hz decision tick** (PA Core Rule 2; supersedes the concept doc's 1–4 Hz estimate — round-22 I7 cross-reference refresh); disturbance system responds synchronously on the server and is cadence-agnostic.
- **`searchRadius` safe range.** Predator AI is responsible for bounding `searchRadius` to a value that the spatial grid can serve efficiently. Recommended bounds: 32 ≤ `searchRadius` ≤ 512 studs.
